=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For integers l, r, let [l, r] denote the set of all integers from l through r. That is, [l, r] = \lbrace l, l+1, l+2, \ldots, r-1, r\rbrace.
You are given N pairs of integers (L_1, R_1), (L_2, R_2), \ldots, (L_N, R_N).
Based on these pairs, consider an undirected graph G defined as follows:

- It has N vertices numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N.
- For all i, j \in [1, N], there is an undirected edge between vertices i and j if and only if the intersection of [L_i, R_i] and [L_j, R_j] is empty.

In addition, for each i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, define the weight of vertex i to be W_i.
You are given Q queries about G. Process these queries in the order they are given.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, Q, the i-th query is the following:

You are given integers s_i and t_i (both between 1 and N, inclusive) such that s_i \neq t_i. Determine whether there exists a path from vertex s_i to vertex t_i in G. If it exists, print the minimum possible weight of such a path.

Here, the weight of a path from vertex s to vertex t is defined as the sum of the weights of the vertices on that path (including both endpoints s and t).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
W_1 W_2 \cdots W_N
L_1 R_1
L_2 R_2
\vdots
L_N R_N
Q
s_1 t_1
s_2 t_2
\vdots
s_Q t_Q

Output

Print Q lines.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, Q, on the i-th line, if there exists a path from vertex s_i to vertex t_i, print the minimum possible weight of such a path, and print -1 otherwise.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq Q \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq W_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq L_i \leq R_i \leq 2N
- 1 \leq s_i, t_i \leq N
- s_i \neq t_i
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5
5 1 4 2 2
2 4
1 2
7 8
4 5
2 7
3
1 4
4 3
5 2

Sample Output 1

11
6
-1

G is a graph with four undirected edges: \lbrace 1, 3\rbrace, \lbrace 2, 3\rbrace, \lbrace 2, 4\rbrace, \lbrace 3, 4\rbrace.

- For the first query, there is a path from vertex 1 to vertex 4 given by 1 \to 3 \to 4. The weight of this path is W_1 + W_3 + W_4 = 5 + 4 + 2 = 11, and this is the minimum possible.
- For the second query, there is a path from vertex 4 to vertex 3 given by 4 \to 3. The weight of this path is W_4 + W_3 = 2 + 4 = 6, and this is the minimum possible.
- For the third query, there is no path from vertex 5 to vertex 2. Hence, print -1.

Sample Input 2

8
44 75 49 4 78 79 12 32
5 13
10 16
6 8
6 15
12 15
5 7
1 15
1 2
5
5 6
3 2
7 5
4 5
5 4

Sample Output 2

157
124
-1
114
114

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus shortest path weight between s,t:
- If intervals disjoint: answer W_s + W_t (direct edge). Since weights positive, direct edge is minimum.

So we need answer queries:
- If intervals s,t disjoint: answer W_s + W_t (direct edge). But could there be a path with more vertices but lower weight than direct? Direct edge weight includes only endpoints; any other path includes endpoints plus positive intermediates, so direct is minimum. Yes.
- If intervals s,t overlap:
  - No direct edge.
  - Length 2 path: s - x - t where x is disjoint from both s and t. Since s,t overlap, x must be either left of both (R_x < min(L_s,L_t)) or right of both (L_x > max(R_s,R_t)). Are there length 3 paths with pattern a left of s, b left of t? Directions? s-a (left), a-b? If b left of a? That would be second move left, same direction, not shortest? Thus for overlapping s,t, the minimum path weight is:
ans = min(
  W_s + W_t + min( minW_left_both, minW_right_both ),  # length2
  W_s + W_t + min( minW_left_of_s + minW_right_of_t, minW_right_of_s + minW_left_of_t )  # length3
)
where:
minW_left_both = min W_i over R_i < min(L_s,L_t)
minW_right_both = min W_i over L_i > max(R_s,R_t)
minW_left_of_s = min W_i over R_i < L_s
minW_right_of_t = min W_i over L_i > R_t
minW_right_of_s = min W_i over L_i > R_s
minW_left_of_t = min W_i over R_i < L_t

But wait length2 x disjoint from both: if x left of both, condition R_x < min(L_s,L_t). If x right of both, L_x > max(R_s,R_t). Since intervals closed and integer, yes.

Length3 pattern 1: a left of s: R_a < L_s; b right of t: L_b > R_t. Need also a-b edge: R_a < L_b. As argued, R_a < L_s <= R_t < L_b because s,t overlap implies L_s <= R_t? Is L_s <= R_t always if overlap? Yes if s starts before or at t ends. But if L_s could be > R_t? Then no overlap. So yes. Thus automatic.
Pattern 2: a right of s: L_a > R_s; b left of t: R_b < L_t. Need b-a edge: R_b < L_a. Since R_b < L_t <= R_s < L_a (overlap implies L_t <= R_s), automatic.

Now, is it possible that a length 3 path uses a or b equal to s or t? Conditions exclude: a left of s cannot be s; b right of t cannot be t; etc. So distinct.

Thus answer for overlapping connected pair can be computed from these prefix/suffix minima over intervals sorted by R and L.

But wait: What if s,t overlap but there is no length2 or length3 path, yet graph connected via longer path? We argued any longer path can be shortened to <=3. But if no length2/3 path, then no path. Let's formalize the shortening lemma:

Let P = v0=s, v1, ..., vk=t be a path in G with k>=2 (since s,t overlap no edge k=1). Among all paths, choose one with minimum number of edges? Let's prove directly for any path, not necessarily alternating, by considering first and last "extreme" maybe.

Alternative proof:
Take a path v0=s,...,vk=t. If there exist i<j-1 with v_i and v_j disjoint, then we can shortcut? Not necessarily if path segment? This is stronger: all non-consecutive vertices overlap. In particular, v0=s overlaps v2,...,v_{k-1}? Actually non-consecutive means not adjacent. Since s and t overlap (given), and s may overlap all vertices except v1? In a shortest path, s overlaps v2,v3,...? If s disjoint from v_j for j>=2, then edge s-v_j would shortcut. So yes, in a shortest path, s overlaps all v_j for j>=2. Similarly t overlaps all v_j for j<=k-2.

Now, because v1 is adjacent to s, either v1<s or s<v1. Suppose v1<s (left). Since v1 is not adjacent to v2? Actually v1 and v2 are adjacent, so disjoint. Since v1<s, v2 cannot be left of v1? Similarly, by induction, directions alternate in a shortest path. Good.

Then for k>3:
Case k even: last direction p_k = opposite p1. If p1=-, p_k=+, so v1<s and v_{k-1}<t. Since s,t overlap, either L_s <= L_t or L_t <= L_s. If L_s <= L_t, v1<s => R_v1 < L_s <= L_t, so v1 left of both, edge s-v1 and v1-t, giving length2. If L_t <= L_s, v_{k-1}<t => R < L_t <= L_s, so v_{k-1} left of both, length2. Case k odd >3: p_k=p1. If p1=-, p_k=-, so v1<s and t<v_{k-1} (v_{k-1} right of t). Thus any path with >3 edges can be shortened to <=3. Therefore min weight path has <=3 edges. Great.

Now, for overlapping s,t, if there is a path of length 2 or 3, formula. If no path, -1. But is it possible that there is a path of length 1? No, overlap no edge. So formula complete.

Now, for disjoint s,t, direct edge length1. Could a length2/3 path have lower weight than direct? Direct includes only W_s+W_t. Any other path includes at least one positive W, so direct is strictly lower. So answer W_s+W_t. No need consider connectivity? Direct edge exists, so connected.

Thus the problem reduces to computing for each query:
If R_s < L_t or R_t < L_s (disjoint): ans = W_s + W_t.
Else (overlap):
  Let A = minW over intervals with R < min(L_s,L_t) (left of both)
  Let B = minW over intervals with L > max(R_s,R_t) (right of both)
  len2 = W_s + W_t + min(A,B) (if INF skip)
  Let C = minW over intervals with R < L_s (left of s)
      D = minW over intervals with L > R_t (right of t)
      E = minW over intervals with L > R_s (right of s)
      F = minW over intervals with R < L_t (left of t)
  len3 = W_s + W_t + min(C + D, E + F)
  ans = min(len2, len3) if finite else -1.

Need be careful: The length3 pattern 1 uses a left of s and b right of t. What if a and b are the same? We argued impossible. But what if a = t? Condition R_t < L_s? Since s,t overlap, R_t >= L_s, so no. b=s? L_s > R_t? no. So distinct.

What if a or b is s/t? no.

Need precompute:
- minW_R_less[x] = min W_i for intervals with R_i < x, for x coordinate maybe 1..2N+1. Since L,R in [1,2N]. We need query R < value. Let prefix over R coordinate: prefR[x] = min W_i with R_i < x? - minW_L_greater[x] = min W_i for intervals with L_i > x. Suffix over L coordinate: suffL[x] = min_{l > x} minL[l]. Query L > R_t. R_t up to 2N; if R_t=2N, none.

Then:
left_both = prefR[min(L_s,L_t)] (since R < minL)
right_both = suffL[max(R_s,R_t)] (since L > maxR)
left_s = prefR[L_s]
right_t = suffL[R_t]
right_s = suffL[R_s]
left_t = prefR[L_t]

Need include all vertices including s,t in these minima? For length2/3, x/a/b cannot be s/t due conditions, but if s/t satisfy condition? For prefR[L_s], does s have R_s < L_s? No because L_s<=R_s. But for disjoint query, direct edge. If intervals just touch? Disjoint if R_i < L_j or R_j < L_i. If R_i = L_j, intersection nonempty (integer includes L_j), so overlap. Our overlap condition should be not (R_s < L_t or R_t < L_s). Equivalently R_s >= L_t and R_t >= L_s. Use that.

We included both patterns. What about length3 path where s-a-b-t and s is between a and b? Let's enumerate all possible order relations for three edges with alternating directions.

Let p1 = direction s->a. If p1=-: a < s. p2 must +: a < b. p3 must -: t < b. So b right of t. That's pattern1 (a left of s, b right of t). If p1=+: s < a. p2 must -: b < a. p3 must +: b < t. So a right of s, b left of t. Pattern2. There are no other alternating length3 patterns. Good.

Now, length2 path: s-x-t. Since s,t overlap, x disjoint from both. If x left of s, because s,t overlap, is x necessarily left of t? Not always? Wait for length2, x must be disjoint from both s and t. Good. Similarly right of both.

Now, length3 pattern1: a left of s, b right of t. We don't require a disjoint from t or b disjoint from s. But path s-a-b-t: s-a edge (a left of s), a-b edge (a left of b), b-t edge (b right of t). We showed a-b edge automatic from a left of s and b right of t because s,t overlap. Let's verify with endpoint order if s,t overlap but L_s could be > R_t? No. If a left of s: R_a < L_s. b right of t: L_b > R_t. Overlap implies L_s <= R_t. Therefore R_a < L_s <= R_t < L_b, so R_a < L_b. Yes. So any such a,b valid. We don't need a != b; impossible as noted.

Pattern2: a right of s (L_a > R_s), b left of t (R_b < L_t). Overlap implies L_t <= R_s, so R_b < L_t <= R_s < L_a, so b left of a, edge. Good.

Now, could there be a length3 path with p1=- but b not right of t? Directions force p3=- means t < b (b right of t). Yes.

Now, query processing:
Read Q.
For each query s,t:
  ws=W[s], wt=W[t]
  if R[s] < L[t] or R[t] < L[s]:
      ans = ws + wt
  else:
      minLboth = prefR[min(L[s], L[t])]
      minRboth = suffL[max(R[s], R[t])]
      best = INF
      if minLboth < INF and minRboth < INF? Actually min of them; if one INF, other finite, length2 exists. best2 = ws + wt + min(minLboth, minRboth). If min is INF, skip. Since min could be INF if both INF. Use if m2 < INF.
      # length3
      c = prefR[L[s]]
      d = suffL[R[t]]
      e = suffL[R[s]]
      f = prefR[L[t]]
      # Need c+d finite, e+f finite
      if c < INF and d < INF: best = min(best, ws+wt+c+d)
      if e < INF and f < INF: best = min(best, ws+wt+e+f)
      ans = best if best < INF else -1
  print(ans)

Now, let's revisit the shortening lemma with vertex weights: We choose a minimum weight path. If it has >3 edges, we can produce a valid path with fewer vertices, hence lower weight because all weights positive and removed at least one vertex. But our shortcut might produce a path that repeats vertices? It uses subset of original vertices, no repeats if original simple. Even if original has repeats, we can remove cycles first. Minimum weight path can be simple (positive weights), so no repeats. The shortcut path uses v0, v1, v_{k-1}, vk for odd; these are distinct? v1 could equal v_{k-1} if k=3? For k>3, v1 and v_{k-1} are distinct because simple path. For even shortcut uses v0, v_j, vk where j=1 or k-1; distinct. Good.

But wait: For odd path shortening to length3, we need v1 and v_{k-1} are disjoint. We proved if p1=-. But what if the path directions alternate but p1=- and p_k=-, yes v1<s and t<v_{k-1}. Since s,t overlap, R_v1 < L_s <= R_t < L_v_{k-1}. This uses L_s <= R_t. Overlap condition includes L_s <= R_t. Good. If p1=+, symmetric: v1>s and v_{k-1}<t. Then R_v_{k-1} < L_t <= R_s < L_v1, so v_{k-1}<v1, edge. Path s-v1-v_{k-1}-t? Let's check order: s < v1, v_{k-1} < t, and v_{k-1} < v1. Edges: s-v1 (s<v1), v1-v_{k-1} (v_{k-1}<v1), v_{k-1}-t (v_{k-1}<t). Valid. Good.

For even path shortening to length2: If p1=-, p_k=+, so v1<s and v_{k-1}<t. If L_s <= L_t, v1 left of both; path s-v1-t. If L_t <= L_s, v_{k-1} left of both; path s-v_{k-1}-t. Need ensure v1 or v_{k-1} not equal s/t? v1<s so not s; v1 left of both implies R_v1 < L_t, so v1 != t (since L_t <= R_t). v_{k-1}<t so not t; left of both implies R < L_s, so not s. Good. If p1=+, symmetric with right of both. Thus any even path can be shortened to length2. What about k=2? Already length2. For k>2 even, yes.

Thus min weight path length <=3.

Now, one subtlety: The graph is undirected, and our "directions alternate" in a shortest path by number of vertices, but for a minimum weight path, could it have two consecutive edges in same direction but still be minimum weight because the shortcut vertex has negative? Weights positive, so shortcut removing a vertex always lowers weight. If two consecutive edges same direction, say u<v<w, then u and w are disjoint (R_u < L_v <= R_v < L_w), so edge u-w exists. Then path can skip v, lower weight. Thus min weight path has no consecutive same direction. Good.

Now, let's consider if a path with repeated vertices could have lower weight? No positive weights, cycles increase.

If a and b are distinct but what if a=b? no. So independent.

Now, let's think about the graph's connected components. Our formula for overlapping s,t returns -1 if no length2/3. But what if s,t overlap and there is a path of length 4 but no length2/3? Now, let's think about "intersection empty" for intervals with integer endpoints. If R_i < L_j, empty. If R_i = L_j, intersection contains L_j, so not empty. Our strict inequalities correct. Prefix R < L uses strict. Suffix L > R uses strict. Good.

Implementation plan:
import sys
def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    W = [0]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1): W[i]=next(it)
    L=[0]*(N+1); R=[0]*(N+1)
    M=2*N
    INF=10**30
    minR=[INF]*(M+2)  # index 0..M+1
    minL=[INF]*(M+2)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        l=next(it); r=next(it)
        L[i]=l; R[i]=r
        if W[i] < minR[r]: minR[r]=W[i]
        if W[i] < minL[l]: minL[l]=W[i]
    prefR=[INF]*(M+2)
    # prefR[x] = min R < x
    for x in range(1, M+2):
        # prefR[x] = min(prefR[x-1], minR[x-1])
        v = prefR[x-1]
        if minR[x-1] < v: v = minR[x-1]
        prefR[x]=v
    suffL=[INF]*(M+2)
    # suffL[x] = min L > x
    for x in range(M, -1, -1):
        v = suffL[x+1]
        if minL[x+1] < v: v = minL[x+1]
        suffL[x]=v
    Q=next(it)
    out=[]
    for _ in range(Q):
        s=next(it); t=next(it)
        ws=W[s]; wt=W[t]
        if R[s] < L[t] or R[t] < L[s]:
            out.append(str(ws+wt))
        else:
            best=INF
            m2 = prefR[min(L[s], L[t])]
            v = suffL[max(R[s], R[t])]
            if v < m2: m2 = v
            if m2 < INF:
                val = ws + wt + m2
                if val < best: best=val
            c = prefR[L[s]]
            d = suffL[R[t]]
            if c < INF and d < INF:
                val = ws + wt + c + d
                if val < best: best=val
            e = suffL[R[s]]
            f = prefR[L[t]]
            if e < INF and f < INF:
                val = ws + wt + e + f
                if val < best: best=val
            if best < INF:
                out.append(str(best))
            else:
                out.append("-1")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
if __name__ == "__main__": solve()

Now, let's consider if there is a path of length 3 where a left of s and b right of t, but a-b edge automatic, but if a and b are not distinct? no.

Ok.

Now, let's think about if there is a path of length 3 where a left of s and b right of t, but a-b
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)

    N = next(it)
    W = [0] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        W[i] = next(it)

    L = [0] * (N + 1)
    R = [0] * (N + 1)

    M = 2 * N
    INF = 10**30

    minR = [INF] * (M + 2)  # min weight by R_i
    minL = [INF] * (M + 2)  # min weight by L_i

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        l = next(it)
        r = next(it)
        L[i] = l
        R[i] = r
        if W[i] < minR[r]:
            minR[r] = W[i]
        if W[i] < minL[l]:
            minL[l] = W[i]

    # prefR[x] = min weight of interval with R_i < x
    prefR = [INF] * (M + 2)
    for x in range(1, M + 2):
        v = prefR[x - 1]
        if minR[x - 1] < v:
            v = minR[x - 1]
        prefR[x] = v

    # suffL[x] = min weight of interval with L_i > x
    suffL = [INF] * (M + 2)
    for x in range(M, -1, -1):
        v = suffL[x + 1]
        if minL[x + 1] < v:
            v = minL[x + 1]
        suffL[x] = v

    Q = next(it)
    ans = []

    for _ in range(Q):
        s = next(it)
        t = next(it)

        ws = W[s]
        wt = W[t]

        # Direct edge
        if R[s] < L[t] or R[t] < L[s]:
            ans.append(str(ws + wt))
            continue

        best = INF

        # Length 2: one vertex disjoint from both s and t
        m2 = prefR[min(L[s], L[t])]
        v = suffL[max(R[s], R[t])]
        if v < m2:
            m2 = v
        if m2 < INF:
            val = ws + wt + m2
            if val < best:
                best = val

        # Length 3 pattern 1: a is left of s, b is right of t
        c = prefR[L[s]]
        d = suffL[R[t]]
        if c < INF and d < INF:
            val = ws + wt + c + d
            if val < best:
                best = val

        # Length 3 pattern 2: a is right of s, b is left of t
        e = suffL[R[s]]
        f = prefR[L[t]]
        if e < INF and f < INF:
            val = ws + wt + e + f
            if val < best:
                best = val

        if best < INF:
            ans.append(str(best))
        else:
            ans.append("-1")

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
